- Demystifying Digital Ads: How to Win the Meta Auction | Keystone Digital Hub #digitalmarketing: Kind: captions Language: en-US Let's be honest, have you ever felt like your phone is listening to you? Well, today we're going to pull back the curtain on the powerful, and let's face it, kind of mysterious, world of digital ads to see what's really going on. You know the feeling. You're just scrolling through your feed, minding your own business, and BAM! There's an ad for something you were just thinking about. Is it magic? A crazy coincidence? Or is there a seriously complex system working behind the scenes? Well, that's exactly what we're going to break down. Okay, so to really get how these ads work, we have to take one tiny step back and talk about what marketing even is, because it's not just about selling stuff. At its heart, it's about one single powerful idea. Right, here's the official definition from the American Marketing Association. I know, no, it sounds a little academic, but hang with me. Just focus on that very last word, value. That one word is the key to this whole entire thing. This little diagram just nails it. You see, whether a business is creating a product, communicating a message, delivering it to you, or exchanging it for money, every single one of these steps is all about providing value. If that value isn't there, the whole system just falls apart. Now, for decades, getting that message of value out there was pretty straightforward, but it was also super inefficient. And then, well, the internet came along and completely changed the game. And this just shows the shift perfectly. Over on the left, you've got a classic newspaper ad. It's basically shouting one message to thousands of people, just hoping it connects with a few. But on the right, that's your social media feed. That's not shouting, that's a conversation. It's a message that's been tailored, targeted, and delivered directly to you. So in today's digital world, we often look at it through this thing called the PISO model. You've got your owned media, which is stuff like a company's own website. Then there's shared media. That's all the user-generated content on social platforms. And earned media is like getting a great review or a news article written about you. But today, we are zeroing in on that fourth piece of the pie, paid media. This is the engine that makes social media go round, and trust me, it's where things get really, really interesting. So, how in the world does a platform like Meta decide which of the millions of ads out there to show you at any given second? Well, it all comes down to this lightning-fast, super high-stakes auction. I love this analogy. Facebook is an auction house. Think about that. Every single time you open your feed, an auction is happening in milliseconds, with thousands of advertisers all bidding for your attention. So who wins? It's probably not who you think. At the heart of this whole auction, you've got this, well, this tug of war. The advertiser, naturally, wants to show you their ad first, and they wanna pay as little as possible. But Meta's goal, it's to show you ads that are actually relevant to you, ads that create a positive experience. Why? Because that's what keeps you on the platform. To balance all this out, Meta's algorithm creates what you can think of as a winner's bracket. And to even get in the running, an ad needs three things. It needs a competitive bid, sure. It also has to be super relevant to its audience. And most importantly, it has to have high ad quality. And here is the real secret. You just can't buy your way to the top. The most important factor, and the one that advertisers have the most control over, is ad quality. This is exactly how a small business with a fantastic ad can beat a giant corporation with a massive budget. So what is this ad quality score anyway? It's not just one thing. It's actually a mix of three different rankings that Meta is calculating for every single ad that goes through its system. Let's break down these three pillars, because once you understand these, you'll understand exactly why you see the ads that you do. It all starts here with the quality ranking and this is a direct comparison. Meta literally looks at your ads, the image, the video, the words, and it judges its quality against all the other ads that are trying to reach the very same people. Now, An ad's quality score can drop for some pretty obvious reasons, you know, like blurry images or bad grammar. But it also gets dinged for more subtle things, like negative comments, or if the ad links to a website that's so slow, people immediately click away, which Meta can totally track. Even showing the same ad to the same person way too many times will hurt its score. Next up is the engagement rate ranking. This one is all about action. Is your ad interesting enough to make people actually stop scrolling and do something? Likes, comments, shares, clicks, all of these are signals to meta that, hey, people actually find this interesting. And engagement just plummets when an ad is shown to the wrong people, or if the content is, well, just boring. A weak call to action, or an ad that just feels way too pushy and salesy is gonna make people scroll right on by. And the third piece, which you could argue is the most important part of the whole puzzle, is the conversion rate ranking. This is the ultimate test. After someone clicks your ad, do they actually follow through? If your ad promised a great deal on shoes, do they actually buy the shoes? This tells Meta if your ad really delivered on its promise. Think about it. An ad can have amazing pictures and get tons of clicks, but that conversion rate will tank if the person lands on a confusing website or has to deal with a super complicated checkout, or if they discover the prices are just way too high. And a huge one these days, if the site doesn't work well on a phone, that's a deal breaker. Okay, I know that was a lot of theory. So how does this all come together in the real world? What does a winning ad actually look like? Let's run through a quick cheat sheet. To really succeed, an ad has to have it all. You need an eye-catching creative just to stop the scroll. The text needs to be clear, simple, and get straight to the point. The headline has to scream what makes the product unique. Using the right call to action button is also huge, like choosing shop now instead of learn more if you want a sale. And finally, that positive social proof, like great comments, that builds trust, and trust is everything. In the end, it all comes back to that big shift we talked about, from broadcasting to conversation. An ad isn't just a billboard anymore, it's the start of a dialogue. And in this world of endless digital noise, the algorithms are designed to reward those who have something valuable, engaging, and honest to say. So the real question is, are you saying the right thing?

As in, with the one adset that's set up for cold audiences, you get the same split as with the other adset set up for warm audiences. They're just trying to go out and get you results. They're using your inputs and suggestions and doing their own thing. And that makes it silly to have separate adsets or separate campaigns for cold and warm audiences where Meta is going to target a mixture of the two anyway. Right? So, that's one of the big changes that we're now making is that there are still some rare circumstances where we will separate them out if we really want to change the messaging between different stages of a salesunnel, for example. But that's rare. By default, we're absolutely going to combine both cold and warm audiences into the same campaign and the same adset. So, the next thing we're trying to do with the new campaign structure is consolidate as much as possible. And that applies to both campaigns and adsets. We're going to try and run as few campaigns and as few adsets as possible. Often just using the one campaign and the one adset. I'll explain later on when we do and don't want to uh to do that. Why are we doing that? Well, it's best to combine the data into as few elements as possible. Let's say you generate 70 conversions in a week. Instead of having that spread across 10 different adsets, so seven conversions roughly per adset per week, Meta is going to struggle to optimize those adsets. Going to struggle to get out of the learning phase. Whereas, if that's in one adset, you're generating 70 conversions per week in that one adset. Meta is able to fully optimize. They're able to work out the best time of day to advertise, what ad impression frequency is required, advertise to this person instead of this person, use this ad instead of this ad. all the various points that Meta's system optimizes to get us more of what we want, more sales, more leads, whatever it is we've set up our campaign to optimize for, Meta can do that better with more conversion data. So much better to have that consolidated data into the one campaign, one adset as opposed to spread across lots of different elements that will struggle uh to optimize. Second reason why we want to consolidate is because we want to avoid auction overlap. Now, there's some misconceptions around overlap. A lot of people think I'm talking about audience overlap. When I say auction overlap, I'm not audience overlap would be you've got two different campaigns, two different adsets, and they're targeting a lot of the same audience. Now, that's not ideal, but the whole idea of that's going to increase the cost because you're bidding against yourself is a bit silly. There are thousands of advertisers trying to reach the same people. One extra campaign ad setting that not going to make much difference. But you can run into issues with auction overlap, which is a different thing. Meta systems are now very sophisticated at working out what ad impression frequency is required to get the outcome that you want. So Meta might work out, you know what, we're better off at generating four or five ad impressions for a single individual over a 24 48 hour time period as opposed to spreading out those ad impressions over a week. Well, Meta could plan that really well if it's just got the one campaign, one adset targeting a person, a group of people obviously, but we're talking about one individual user in this case, but targeting um a group of people. If you've got some ad impressions coming from one adset, some ad impressions coming from another adset or different campaigns, much more difficult for Meta to work out that delivery system of right, let's put an ad in front of this person four times over the next 48 hours. Much more difficult for them to be able to uh to do that. So, that's that's the issue that comes up with auction overlap. and you want to take advantage of what Meta's delivery system um is able to do. Okay. So, the next thing we've changed is that we very rarely now test different audiences at the adset or even the campaign level. For the same reason that we don't separate those out, meta is kind of going to ignore a lot of your suggestions. They're going to do their own thing when it comes to targeting anyway. is why we won't test different interest targeting options in different adsets or interest targeting versus lookalike versus open targeting. We used to do a lot of that sort of stuff. We don't do that anymore because all of that falls within the suggested audience section unless it's going to do its own thing. Um, so it might be that a business just advertises in one country, that's who they can serve and we're happy just to advertise to the entire country. We're not going to bother testing that. It's not a coincidence that we will test different um targeting options when it comes to a location basis across different adsets, maybe different campaigns and the location targeting falls within the control section. Basically, we are able to do that testing accurately on a location basis whereas we aren't with say different interests or interests versus lookalikes and stuff like that because the control section within targeting here those are hard boundaries. They're not going to treat that as a suggestion and go beyond it where they think it'll help you get um your desired outcome. Another reason why we're able to consolidate and use fewer campaigns, fewer adsets, is because Meta can now handle a lot more ads within an adset. So, they used to have a recommendation of don't have five or six ads live in an adset at any one time. It was basically too many variables for the system. That has now changed with Andromeda. with some other updates. Meta is able to handle a lot more ad creative and different ads within um an adset. This person seems to respond to product demonstration style ads. We're going to put the more of those in front of them. If they have the different ads and the different types of creative within the one ad set, Meta can start to do a better job of personalizing ad delivery and working out that it's not a one-sizefits-all. There are different people within your target audience and different people respond um to different um creative. So that's one of the big big shifts that have come from the change in the platform, the updated campaign structure is a lot more ads within an adset. I don't worry about an upper limit anymore. The constraining factor is usually how many good quality ads can be created. What are the resources available?   7. @lemonroti1991: Consolidation is key: The modern recommended approach is to minimize the number of campaigns and ad sets. Instead of separating cold and warm audiences, it is now more effective to combine them into a single ad set (0:23 - 0:51, 5:12 - 6:21).

Leveraging Meta’s algorithm: Because Meta’s targeting systems use your inputs as suggestions rather than strict rules, they are highly capable of automatically optimizing delivery for better results when provided with more consolidated conversion data (1:05 - 2:43).

Creative Diversity: With the platform's increased sophistication, it is recommended to include a wide variety of ad formats (videos, images, UGC, etc.)—often 20 or more—within a single ad set, allowing the system to personalize delivery to different users (10:10 - 12:18).

Testing strategy: The video advises testing ad copy variations (headlines and primary text) directly within ad units rather than creating separate ads. Location-based targeting remains an exception where testing in separate ad sets is still beneficial (8:27 - 10:09, 12:20 - 13:45).

Automatic Sequencing: By consolidating, you allow Meta to automatically perform "ad sequencing," showing top-of-funnel content to new prospects and retargeting with lower-funnel content like testimonials, all within the same ad set (15:00 - 18:49).

When you should deviate from this structure (e.g., for specific product ranges, high-ticket services, or specific content strategies) is also addressed toward the end of the video (18:50 - 23:29).

- The Meta Ad Auction: Kind: captions Language: en-US Buying ads on Meta isn't like buying ads on television. You don't just pay to buy commercial space. The Meta ad system is built off what is called an auction system. And in order to understand how Meta charges for ads, it's important to understand how the auction system works first. The Meta ad auction is how Meta decides how much to charge for an ad. And the ad auction is designed to achieve two goals, creating value for advertisers by helping them reach and get results from people in their target audience, while also providing positive, relevant experiences for people who use the Meta products. To achieve this balance, they hold an auction to match the right ad to the right person at the right time. Each time there's an opportunity to show an ad to someone, an auction takes place to determine which ad to show to that person. And billions of auctions take place every day across the Meta family of apps. So who competes in these auctions? When an advertiser like you creates an ad, you define your target audience on Meta. But what happens when another advertiser wants to target the same audiences to you? Then both you and the other advertiser compete in the auction to show that person your ad. For example, say one advertiser targets all women who like flowers. Another advertiser targets all flower lovers who live in California. In that case, the same woman in California who likes flowers could fall into both of these advertisers' audiences. Let's call this woman Clara. So how does Meta decide whose ad Clara sees? Meta decides which ad Clara sees by calculating a total value for each ad, which is effectively a numerical score. The ad with a higher total value wins the auction and gets shown to Clara. So what does this calculation look like? An ad's total value is based on three things. The advertiser bid, the estimated action rate, and the ad quality. We'll explain each of them briefly. The advertiser bid is how much an advertiser is willing to pay to achieve their desired outcome, like a website visit or a newsletter signup. You can manually set that bid, or you can let Meta automatically determine what your bid should be. The more money you bid, the higher your advertiser bid will be. It's good to remember, though, that the winner of an auction is not simply the advertiser that pays the most. It's a combination of all the factors in the equation. The next part of the total value equation is the estimated action rate. The estimated action rate measures the likelihood that a certain ad shown to a certain person will lead to a desired outcome. It's basically an estimation of how likely the user is to fulfill your ad campaign objective, whether that's pressing a Shop Now button or going to your website. The final part of the total value equation is ad quality. Ad quality is determined by many sources, including feedback from people that view or hide your ad, as well as findings of low-quality attributes, such as sensationalized language, engagement bait, and more. Together, the advertiser bid, the estimated action rate, and the ad quality make up an ad's total value. So what does this mean for Clara, who is being targeted by two flower companies? Let's assume both companies run an ad with the goal of getting people to purchase flowers from their online website. They input the same advertiser bid, and Meta determines Clara is equally likely to fulfill the desired goal to buy products from them. This means the estimated action rate is the same for both ads, too. Advertiser A, however, is sending people to a website that has a lot of ads on their homepage, as well as misleading information about flower pricing. That's not a great user experience. Hypothetically, this could lower Advertiser A's ad quality score, driving the total value down. And this means Advertiser B would have a higher total value score, leading to them winning the auction and Clara seeing their ad. So now that you understand the auction, how does that relate to how much you're charged for an ad? Billions of auctions take place every day across the Meta family of ads, and often with far more than two companies. Every time you see an ad, it's the result of an auction, and Meta only charges you for those ads when you win the auction. Meta charges you by the number of times your ad is displayed to someone, also known as an impression. Meta charges on a 1,000 impression basis, also known as CPM, or cost per thousand. CPM measures the total amount spent on an ad campaign, divided by impressions, multiplied by 1,000. Here's an example. If you spent $50 on your ad and got 10,000 impressions, your CPM would be $5. You might be wondering why CPM is important. CPM is a common metric used by the online advertising industry to gauge the cost-effectiveness of an ad campaign. It's often used to compare performance among different ad publishers and campaigns. So while you get charged $50, you can gauge what you were charged for by the CPM price. For some marketing goals, you can also choose to be charged per link click or per action. This means Meta will only charge you if a customer clicks on your ad link, or take another specific action, such as watching a video. Now that you know how the auction works and how Meta charges for ads, you're ready to set your budget in Ads Manager. Your Meta ad budget is the total amount you want to spend daily or over the course of the campaign. Meta will try to spend your budget evenly throughout the time your ad is running. You won't be charged more than the budget you set. So if you set a maximum budget of $100 to run an ad over a month, but Facebook only spends $70 that month based on the ad's performance, your bill will be $70. Remember, when you're setting your budget for Meta ads, it's the maximum amount you're willing to spend, not the actual amount spent. Finally, let's briefly cover how you will actually pay for your ads. And in general, there are two ways you may be charged for ads. Many advertisers have what's called automatic payments. With automatic payments, Meta will automatically charge you whenever you spend a certain amount, which is known as a billing threshold. You'll be charged each time you hit your billing threshold and again on a monthly bill date for any leftover charges. You can also pay via manual payments. With manual payments, you'll preload money to your account before your ads run. And after that, Meta will typically deduct from that amount up to once a day as your ads run. For smaller budgets, Meta may wait until the charges grow to a certain amount or until seven days pass before they invoice your account.

- The Truth About Meta Awareness Campaigns (Most Get This Wrong): Kind: captions Language: en If you're running meta awareness campaigns to build brand awareness, stop. Because in most cases, it does almost nothing for your brand. Yes, the CPMs look amazing. You can reach hundreds of thousands of people for very little money. But there's a reason those impressions are so cheap. And once you understand where Meta is currently showing those ads and how they measure something called ad recall lift, you might start questioning whether those impressions have any real value at all to your business. So in this video, I'll show you why we almost never run awareness campaigns and the two campaign types that actually build brand awareness effectively instead. So before I explain why awareness campaigns are rarely a good idea, I'll quickly explain that building brand awareness via meta ads is fantastic. It works really, really well. Having brand awareness makes everything easier for your business. If you look at a typical marketing funnel, the first thing we need to do right at the top is to make people aware of our business because no one can buy from you until they know who you are. So recently I interviewed the CEO of a company called Mick George who sponsored the shirts of Peter reunited Football Club and he said that once they started doing the shirt sponsorship over 10 years ago it made everything in their business easier. More sales, easier recruitment and better partnerships. So I'm not trying to put you off from building brand awareness via Meta Ads. It's the cheapest and best way of doing that via digital channels. However, towards the end of the video, I'm going to explain the better way to do it via other campaign types. So, let's start with why awareness campaigns look so attractive in the first place. And it comes down to the CPM, which is the cost to reach a,000 impressions. When you run awareness campaigns, Meta's objective is simple, to show your ads to as many people as possible for the lowest cost possible. And that's exactly what happens. You'll often see CPMs that are dramatically cheaper than other campaign types, sometimes just a few pounds to reach 1,000 people. So, it's attractive to business owners. So, when advertisers log into their ad account, it looks great. Huge reach, loads of impressions, and all for a very small budget. But the problem is that cheap reach doesn't automatically mean reach. And the reason those impressions are so cheap comes to how Meta's ad auction actually works. So why are those impressions so cheap? Every single placement on Meta operates on a bidding system. Advertisers compete for attention in different environments on the platform. Some impressions are very very valuable. Now for example, showing an ad in the feed of a senior decision maker during the middle of the working day is highly competitive. Lots of advertisers want that placement, which means the cost goes up, supply and demand. But other impressions are less competitive. For example, people scrolling late at night, users in placements that receive very little attention, or areas of the platform where engagement is naturally lower. When you run an awareness campaign, Meta's goal is simply to find the cheapest possible impressions available in the auction. And that's what it does. which means your ads are often delivered in places where attention is low, competition is low, and therefore the price is low. So yeah, you reach a lot of people, but that doesn't necessarily mean those people are really noticing or remembering your brand, which is what it's all about. And this leads to the metric Meta uses to justify awareness campaigns. So Meta uses something called ad recall lift to measure the success of its awareness campaigns. and it's trying to measure how many people would actually remember your brand after seeing your ads. Now, I'm skeptical about how accurate this is. As in our experience, you reach hundreds of thousands of people for very little cost, but there is little to no difference in the revenue, lead generation, or sales that you achieve, which for me means that it doesn't really work. The first option to truly build brand awareness is engagement campaigns, particularly using the video views optimization goal. With this approach, you're no longer optimizing for just impressions. Instead, you're optimizing for people who actually watch part of your video. For example, you can optimize for users who watch at least 25% of your videos or 50, 75, 100% of your video. That might not sound like a huge number, but think about the difference. Someone scrolling past an ad for half a second is very different from somebody watching a meaningful proportion of your video content. If someone watches 25% of a video about your product, service, or brand, they've already spent far more time with your brand than somebody who simply generated an impression in an awareness campaign. That makes video engagement a much stronger signal of real awareness. And we've seen this work really, really well with many, many clients, including a company called Rare Productions who need to build brand awareness with the parents of children who live near their theater locations. Once we began this strategy, they saw their audition booking rate dramatically increase. The second alternative is traffic campaigns. So, we often recommend traffic campaigns. Now, in this case, you're optimizing for people who actually click on your ad and they go through to visit your website. Now, again, this creates a much stronger interaction with your brand. Somebody who takes the step to click on your ad has been hooked into it and they land on your website, which shows real intent and curiosity. But there's one important thing to monitor here, and that's just because somebody clicks on your ad doesn't necessarily mean the visit was valuable. Now, that's why we always recommend looking at the engagement metrics inside Google Analytics 4, specifically things like the average engagement time per visit from that meta traffic, the time spent on page, whether people are interacting with your content, if visitors are landing on your site and leaving after 2 or 3 seconds, that visit didn't really do much for brand awareness. But if the average engagement time is 30 seconds, 45 seconds, or even a minute, then people are actually reading, exploring, and becoming familiar with your brand, you're leaving that lasting impression. And that's exactly what you want when you're trying to build brand awareness, genuine brand awareness. So the key takeaway, while meta awareness campaigns might look appealing because of their extremely cheap CPMs, the reality is that those impressions often come from low attention environments and metrics like ad recall lift are based on estimates rather than actual measurable user behavior. That's why we generally avoid awareness campaigns. Instead, we focus on campaign types that encourage real engagement that is measurable. things like video views, engagement campaigns, or traffic campaigns that bring users to your website. Because when somebody watches your content or spends time on your website, you're not just generating impressions, you're actually building familiarity with your brand, which is going to make everything in your business easier. And that's what real brand awareness is all about. So, I hope that helps. Don't forget to like and subscribe, and we'll see you next time.

But, two things we do need to talk about are the buying types and the campaign objective. So, we've got two different buying types. We've got auction, and we've got reservation. Auction is just the cost of your Facebook and Instagram ads is going to be determined by simple market forces, demand and supply. More demand, higher cost. Less demand, lower cost. And that's demand from advertisers, by the way. How many advertisers there are, how much they're willing to spend. Uh reservation, you can actually guarantee a certain price, often a lower price. And and the cost of Facebook and Instagram advertising is normally measured in CPMs. So, cost per 1,000 impressions. So, it's cheaper to use reservation, but that's not the option I'd recommend you use, which might sound bizarre. Here's why. In order for Meta to be able to guarantee that lower cost, they are going to put your ads in front of lower quality prospects, or lower quality ad placements. The better quality ad impressions are usually seen through auction, and that's a big deal. So, I'd recommend you go with auction. Let's move on to campaign objective. So, you've got six different options here, and they do different things. And your campaign objective is really, really important because it's going to inform how Meta optimizes your campaign. So, you're basically saying to Meta, "This is what I want." And Meta's really powerful machine learning and AI-based systems are going to optimize your ads, work out who to advertise to, what time of day, how often, all those things to get you more of what you want. That's why it's so important to get your campaign objective set up correctly. So, you've got awareness. Awareness is where Meta will put your ads in front of people that are most likely to remember them. Put your ads in front of as many people as possible. Not an option I typically recommend for beginners or for businesses operating with a relatively small Facebook and Instagram ad budget because it can be quite an easy way in those scenarios to spend a bunch of money and not really see anything back for it. What we see with awareness is it can work really well for larger businesses with big budgets because awareness takes time and it takes a lot of ad impressions across a lot of people over an extended period. Bigger businesses have those budgets, smaller ones and beginners to to Meta ads typically don't. There is an exception and that's if you sell a high-ticket or expertise-based service and you might want to use my omnipresent content strategy which typically does use awareness. So, if you meet that criteria, maybe go ahead and and check that out. But otherwise, for most of you watching this, I would say leave awareness alone for now. Then we've got traffic. Traffic is designed to optimize for as many link clicks as possible or as many landing pages as possible to get as many people from Facebook and Instagram to your website. Now, a lot of beginner Facebook and Instagram advertisers use traffic. They think, "Great. I'll send a load of traffic to my website. I want as many people as possible. I'll then generate my leads, sales, etc. from those people." It's not typically the best way to go though if you do really want purchases or you do really want leads that can go on to use your services. There are better options that we'll get to in a minute because Meta's optimization system is very literal. So, if you say to Meta, "I want as many link clicks as possible." Meta is very good at finding people that are likely to click. But those people don't necessarily go on to convert. Other campaign objectives and options might be better at, yes, generating a few less clicks to your website, but the people that do arrive are far more likely to go ahead and take the next action. Sometimes we are forced to use the traffic objective if you cannot optimize, you cannot track a purchase on your website or you cannot track who becomes a lead. Let's say for example, you're sending traffic through to a third-party site where you can't track the actions that people take once they get there. Well, then you may be forced to use traffic. Engagement is what it sounds like, a campaign objective is going to optimize for likes, comments, and video views. Basically, just getting people to engage with the ads themselves. So, leads and sales are much more direct response marketing and and if you select leads, then Meta will optimize your campaign for people who are likely to go on to become a lead. So, once we select our our conversion location, in this case website, that is going to change what options we have down here. The the most important of which being performance goal. So, I talked earlier at the campaign creation stage about how important the campaign objective is in getting that right. You can still mess up a good campaign objective selection with a bad performance goal. Because what the campaign objective selection really does is it determines which options you have later on. The performance goal is how Meta's going to optimize your campaign. So, let me click in here and we can take a look at the options, right? So, the default is maximize number of conversions, but underneath leads goals we also have maximize value of conversions. Now, maximize number of conversions is absolutely fine if you're advertising the one service, and when someone becomes a lead, they look just as valuable as anyone else who becomes a lead. But, if you're advertising multiple services, then it might be that one service, if someone goes on to convert, they're worth $200 to your business. Whereas with another service, if they go on to convert, they're worth $5,000 to your business. There's a massive difference in the value of those two leads depending on what they've said they're interested in. If you go with maximize value of conversions, then Meta is going to not just try and get as many conversions as possible, but optimize and weight it in favor of the more valuable conversions. In other words, you might end up paying more in terms of cost per lead, but that's a trade-off you'd be happy to make if you got more leads for the $5,000 service as opposed to the the $200 service, right? So, this really depends on your business which one you'll go with. If you're just advertising the one service within this campaign, absolutely fine to go with maximize number of conversions. But, if you've got differing services with differing values, definitely go with maximize value of conversions. Now, in order to track that, you're going to need to make sure that you've got all the tracking set up properly. So, you're going to need to have the Meta pixel installed and the values of each lead being sent back to Meta. You're basically giving Meta's targeting algorithms an indication of who you think should see your ads, who your ads should be put in front of, but at the end of the day, it's up to Meta to either go with people within that targeting criteria or find other people that it thinks will are likely to get you more of what we talked about up here, which is maximize number of conversions, lead conversions, basically. - Facebook Ads in 2026: NEW Secrets, Tips & Strategies: Kind: captions Language: en Meta ads have changed again. What worked 12 months ago is already becoming outdated. And if you're still relying on old strategies, you're leaving a lot of money on the table. In this video, I'm going to break down a number of Meta ad strategies and techniques that are working really well right now, including what's changed, what's quietly dominating behind the scenes, and how you can use it to scale profitably. Strategy number one is to use value rules. I'm a big fan of value rules because they allow you to have the best of both worlds when it comes to targeting. With value rules, you can influence who Meta puts your ads in front of without having to overly restrict them through hard targeting constraints. So, you can have the best of both worlds. You can have open targeting and the full flexibility that Meta wants to be able to work out exactly who to put your ads in front of, but give it direction towards your most valuable prospects or customers. Let me quickly show you what this looks like. So, I'm here inside an example Meta ad account and get to the value rule section. I come over here and click on advertising settings, then select value rules, and then go ahead and click on create a rule set. Then I need to select my criteria. Many different options here, conversion location, age, gender, etc., etc. Let's say I go with age. I may have analyzed my customer data and worked out that people over 35 are more valuable to my business. Perhaps they are more likely to convert from lead to eventual client. Perhaps they are more likely to stick on my subscription service for longer. Perhaps they are more likely to buy again from my e-commerce store. Different use cases for different businesses. So, let's say I've identified the demo valuable to my business, select all the age ranges above 35, and then I select by how much I want to increase my bid. Let's say I've worked out those people are 30% more valuable than others. I can go ahead and add in 30 in here, give the rule a name, go ahead and click next, give the rule set a name, 35 plus, click create. I can now add that into my campaigns, add that into my ad set, and Meta will continue to advertise to people that are both over 35 and under 35, but it is going to favor the people that are over 35, be willing to pay more in the auction to reach those people because we've told Meta they're more valuable to my business. So like I said at the beginning, a great way to have the best of both worlds when it comes to targeting, you don't need to have this trade-off between hard constraints but small audience versus open targeting, you can go with this option instead. Strategy number two is to define your audience segments. So Meta breaks down the audience that you're reaching into three different segments. You've got new audience, you've got engaged audience, and you've got existing customers. So it's really important to take a few minutes, it doesn't take very long at all, and get for example your customer list uploaded to your Meta ad account and then define in advertising settings that audience being existing customers. You want to do the same with your engaged audience, which is going to be people that are warm, they've interacted with your business before, but they haven't actually bought. So this could be website visitors, people on your email list but not your customer list, people who've engaged with you across the Meta apps. You can very quickly and easily do this in advertising settings. If we jump back to advertising settings, we've got here in this example ad account these already defined. We've got engaged audience for example, which we defined as our all website visitors 180, and we've got existing customers, we've got an example customer list uploaded. I've separate videos going into more details about how to do that, how to set it up, but really valuable to do because it allows Meta to better allocate budget across cold, warm, and hot audience, which is what these three categories are. You are also able to see in breakdown data when you're looking at your results at your campaign, ad set, or even ad level the split of spend but also where your results are coming from. How many of our purchases are coming from existing customers versus from new audience? What's the cost per purchase look like across those audience? You can do the same with with cost per lead. Takes a few minutes, well worth doing. Number three is to produce way more ad creative. We now aim for 20 different ad creatives per ad set. We used to be limited to six, that's what Meta used to recommend, but post Andromeda things have really changed. Meta is now prioritizing creative diversity. They want lots of different ad creative in the same ad set because it allows them to do a few different things. Firstly, it allows them to personalize ad delivery. So, they're not going to put the same ads in front of everyone. They're not going to work out from your six ads previously, "This is the best performer. Let's put that in front of everyone." Instead, they're going to work out, "This person's probably going to be more interested in this ad, but this person's probably going to be more interested in this ad." Meta obviously has the data to be able to make those calculations, and that's what the Andromeda update is going to help with. So, the more diversity you have amongst your ad creative, so different formats, different styles, different looks, the more you're going to be able to benefit from that. Also, it really helps with ad fatigue. The more creative diversity you have and the more different ads you have in an ad set, the longer it will take for them to fatigue. If someone sees the same ad seven or eight times, even in a two- or three-day period, they might get pretty bored of it. I've seen that. Yeah, get it. Whatever. But, if you've got lots of different ads and Meta is showing one ad in the morning, a different ad in the afternoon, different ad the next morning, etc. to that that one individual, it's going to take much longer before they become bored of it. You get past the I've seen this before, therefore I can scroll on filter much quicker. So, really important to produce extra ad creative. How do you go about doing that? Well, firstly, embrace different formats. Doesn't all have to be video. Video is the best performing ad format for most advertisers, but we want to make sure we've got a range of formats live at any one time. Different people interested in different formats is part of the reason, but also it's much quicker and easier to create lots of say image variations than it is to create lots of extra videos. That's the first thing. Secondly, embrace Meta's variation options. So, when you're uploading your imagery to the ad platform, you go through the creative setup process, Meta is often going to use AI to suggest variations of that image. By all means, take advantage of those. And you can use external tools that use AI to help you with this, too. One of the biggest reasons small budgets fail on Meta isn't targeting, it's the creatives. Because testing properly requires variations and variations cost money. A single UGC video from a creator, hundreds if not thousands of dollars. An agency to consistently produce and test new creatives, thousands if not tens of thousands dollars per month. And that's before you even know if the ads convert. If you're working with a small budget, that math just doesn't work. Hollow flips that part of the equation. You just paste in only your website URL and it analyzes your brand voice, positioning, and visuals. Then generates ready to publish ads, social posts, and email campaigns. But what makes this powerful for small budgets is the variations. Instead of giving you one creative, it can generate batches of different hooks, angles, formats, and layouts in seconds. You can tweak the size, swap visuals, adjust colors, change the messaging, translate it into another language, or even turn a static image into a video without starting from scratch each time. No prompts, no Canva marathons, no waiting weeks on freelancers. And here's why this matters for small budget advertisers. Meta rewards iteration. The people who win aren't always the ones spending the most, they're the ones testing the most. If you can create 10 to 20 creative variations quickly without spending thousands on production, you suddenly have leverage. Instead of spending your budget on creative production, you can spend it on actual ad testing. What used to take hours or cost thousands now takes minutes. For solo founders and small teams, that removes the biggest bottleneck in Facebook ads, producing enough creatives to find a winner. If creative costs have been holding you back from testing properly, Hollow is definitely worth looking at. There is a link in the description and a discount code if you want to try it. Strategy number four is to use partnership ads. Now, this is linked to the previous point I made around creating more ad creative. Partnership ads will certainly help you do that. Just to quickly explain what they are, partnership ads are ads that run from both your account and a creator's account. So, you can hire creators, influencers to produce usually video ad creative promoting your products and services. And then when you run them, they will be run from those two accounts, yours and theirs, hence why it's called a partnership ad. It's effectively influencer marketing, but not reliant on the organic distribution of their posts, but you can actually put budget behind it and advertise and run it to their audience. And there's a number of reasons why you might want to do so. Firstly, it's a quick and easy way to produce a lot more ad creative because you're not doing it yourself. Secondly, if you work with the right people and they have influence over your target market, they have scroll-stopping power. People are more likely to pay attention, so your hook rate improves. That's a big deal. That should not be underestimated. Thirdly, their recommendation carries weight. So, when they say you should go and buy this product or service, if it's someone that is respected in your space, people are more likely to do it as opposed to that recommendation coming from someone they don't know or from someone within your company. Fourthly, partnership ads can really help you build a brand over time because if you work with creators over an extended period of time, the creators that you work with, people will start to associate with your business and your business will take on the values and the things that people think about those creators. Means you need to pick well in the first place, but it can be a great way to build a brand. Also, when you're working with influencers creators, they're normally pretty good at producing creative assets, otherwise they wouldn't have become an influencer or creator in the first place. So, it's a great way to get really high-quality ad creative that a lot of businesses just aren't capable of producing themselves. Now, of course, it costs money to go out and hire these people, but if you choose right, you are more than likely going to recoup the cost pretty quickly in terms of increased ad efficiency. If you take 10% of your advertising budget, for example, and pay that to creators, but that creator makes your ad campaign 40% more effective, that's a great trade. You should do that every time. And I see that come up again and again. And Meta have a really simple free tool that you can use to make finding creators to work with, contacting them really easy. So, if I jump back into my example ad account, I'm in campaigns. If I go ahead and click on all tools, and I can come down here to the Creator Marketplace. And then from within here, I can easily find creators to work with. Meta even gives recommendations around the creators most relevant for you. I've created a whole video around the Creator Marketplace, how it works, how to go through it properly. You can find that on my channel, and go ahead and check that out. I just wanted to quickly show you how easy this process can be. Strategy number five is to use the creative testing tool. A lot of advertisers have run into issues post-Andromeda when it comes to testing new ad creative. I've already talked about wanting a lot more ad creative, so we want to test to find out what's working, what's not. A lot of advertisers are running into problems where they upload new ad creative, add that into an existing ad set, and it just gets no ad spend whatsoever, and it feels pointless, like a complete waste of time. The existing best performers just continue to get all the ad spend. So, I've seen advertisers do all sorts of things with like separate campaigns and testing setups. It can get very complicated very quickly. You can end up with fragmented data, not the way to go. Much better to use the creative testing tool. So, let's go back into my example ad account. I don't have any campaigns in here, so let's just quickly create an example campaign. Let's go with a sales campaign. And I'm just going to jump straight to the ad level, which is where you will find the creative testing tool. Then I'm going to scroll down, and it's just here beneath where you add in your ad creative. And if I click on here, I can click on setup test, and you can test two to five different ads simultaneously. You can tell Meta how much of your budget you want to spend on this test, how long you want this test to run, and then what do you want to compare performance? I would absolutely recommend changing from, say, cost per post engagement to something like cost per lead or cost per purchase, or whatever it is that you're actually optimizing for in the campaign. That might mean you need to add in extra time, extra budget to make it work, etc. Click confirm, and then what you do is this will produce five different test ads. You go into each one of those ads, and you add in the different ad creative, and this will do a proper test. Meta will segment out your audience into five different chunks, and make sure they don't overlap so you can properly test to see ad creative A, how does it perform against ad creative B? If you're producing new ad creative and adding them to your campaigns, your existing campaigns, your existing ad set, you can use this to make sure they actually get spend and you find out if they're working or not or whether you just completely wasted your time and potentially money on on getting those things created. So yeah, big fan of the creative testing tool. Not many advertisers know about it, even fewer use it, but I certainly recommend it. Strategy number six is to not use a separate retargeting campaign or even a separate retargeting ad set. Instead, we like to combine the two and run a hybrid so your ads are being delivered to a combination of warm and cold audiences. Why do we do that? Because no matter how you set up your targeting, it is very likely that Meta is going to target a mixture of cold and warm audiences anyway. I talked in a previous point about defining your audience segment and one of the reasons is so that you can go down into the breakdown information and you can break down your results but also your spend by new audience, engaged audience, and existing customers. When you do that in an ad set that is set up to be a retargeting ad set and then you compare the results to an ad set that is set up to be a cold audience ad set, often you will find that the split in terms of spend between new audience, engaged audience, and existing customers is exactly the same. And why does Meta do this? Because most of your targeting inputs are suggestions. When you set up an ad set to be a warm audience ad set, you add in your warm audiences, your custom audiences as targeting, but you do so as a suggestion in the suggested audience section as opposed to within the controls within the hard targeting constraints. So Meta has the flexibility to target other people. If you're targeting more open, more cold audiences, Meta is absolutely going to find your warm audiences. Those are the people that are most likely to engage with your ads. Those are the people that Meta will quickly identify as most likely to purchase, become a lead, whatever it is you're optimizing for, and that is going to put ads in front of them. So, if the two different campaigns or two different ad sets, one one one cold, are going to be the same from a distribution standpoint anyway, you might as well combine them so that you don't have fragmented data. We consolidate that data. One ad campaign producing 50 conversions a week is much better than two ad campaigns producing 25 conversions a week because getting out of the learning phase, you're not being caught in learning limited, Meta has more data to optimize, you have more data as the advertiser to be able to assess things and make optimization decisions. So, we rarely have separate retargeting campaigns. Now, there are circumstances where we do. I've got another video going into more detail on this that you can check out on my channel, but by default, we're not going to retarget separately. Quick one before we continue. Strategy number seven is to focus your initial acquisition on free {slash} inexpensive products or services, or go to the other end of the spectrum and go high price point, premium luxury, high ticket, high touch sales process. I think the mid-range is often the hardest, particularly to scale. It's where most of your competitors are, so it's difficult differentiate just for starters. But, if you just think about the economics of it, if you're offering something free or inexpensive, you want that to be as cheap and as operationally efficient for your business as possible, something that you can give away. It's just not going to be very difficult for you to deliver on that. Or you want to go to the other extreme where you can have all the bells and whistles and all the high touch points in the sales calls and the proposal documents and the follow-up processes. Because when you actually do get a customer, you cover the cost of all that. It's slow, and it's cumbersome, and it's friction, and people are busy. Whereas on WhatsApp, it's like I could just send a few messages back and forth really quickly, find out whether or not I like this product or service, and then go ahead and make a purchasing decision. - How auction overlap is inflating your Meta ad results — the simple exclusion fix: Kind: captions Language: en A. Now, what a lot of people do is they don't have exclusions in their top of funnel and then they're testing. And as a result of that, you have this overlap. It's called auction overlap. And again, I'm trying to make this very straight to point, but what can happen is, let's say that I'm running three ads that are being tested in this ad set and then another three ads tested in this ad set. Without exclusions, Meta is going to overlap the impressions between the two ad sets. And as a result, what could happen is someone comes from this ad set and they get served, let's say, ad number two. Then they get served ad number three from this ad set and they convert. So, if you're local, enter in the local mile radius you serve. If you serve nationwide, target nationwide. If you only ship to certain countries, only include those countries. You get it. It's targeting cold, new people who've never heard of your brand before. Now that you've got that squared away, there are a couple other audiences you still need to know how to set up and use. So, let's talk about custom audiences for small businesses in 2026. A custom audience is something you set up here in the audiences tab in ads manager, and you can create one based off of your sources or meta sources, depending on what you're trying to do. You can see all the options for custom audiences here. So, custom audiences are built for three main reasons. The first is to target these people directly. This is called retargeting. On average, people need to see a brand seven times before they convert, and you can't get in front of people seven times if you're only targeting new people in a broad campaign all the time. So, you can create a custom audience of website visitors or Facebook engagement or what have you, and exclusively target that audience. The second reason is to exclude this audience. Once you've made the audiences, you'll want to go back to your broad targeting campaign and exclude these people from it so that it acts as a customer acquisition campaign, solely bringing new people into your funnel. You don't want to get to a point where you're solely just retargeting everybody already in your funnel without any new people coming in. Excluding a custom audience from your broad prospecting campaign prevents that from happening. And the third reason is it's all data. And the more data meta has on your audience, the faster it gets at finding new people who are similar. Custom audiences are one of the clearest signals you can give meta about who matters to your business. So, let's take a look at the four types that are usually the best to build at least to start with. Number one are website visitors. These are people who have been on your website within a certain amount of time and are therefore considered a warm audience because they already know your brand and are showing interest. Number two are email lists. Now, this can and should be built out into a few separate audiences. So, like you could have a list of subscribed but haven't purchased yet, or a leads list of emails, and a customer list of emails. Because they're all at different points in the buying cycle, they should be receiving different ads from you. And that's why I would recommend creating a custom audience for each of them instead of lumping them all into one big custom audience. But they all fall under the category of email list for the sake of this video. Number three, video viewers. This is going to be created from meta sources, and it's people who have consumed your content for a certain amount of seconds. And number four, social engagement audiences. People who have engaged with your Instagram or Facebook content in general, like likes, comments, shares, etc. Now, when it comes to using these custom audiences in your targeting, a lot of business owners still build tiny audiences because they think smaller means better. But that is the opposite way of how you should do it now. For example, instead of only retargeting the last 7 days of website traffic, it's often smarter to retarget a larger audience, like all website visitors over a longer window. Why? Because meta already knows the person who visited two days ago is probably hotter than the person who visited five months ago. You do not need to manually force that. Meta can prioritize the hotter prospect first. But if you build the audience too small, you remove other options that still could convert. That's why broader, stronger data pools tend to work better. Not because every person is equally valuable, but because meta is better at sorting through that value than most manual setups. Now, the last audience I want to talk to you about is one that some of you may already be pretty familiar with, and that's a look-alike audience. If you're not familiar, a look-alike audience is an audience of new people that meta finds that look like the source audience of your choosing. So, you could find and target new people who look like your video views, your blog subscribers, or most importantly, your customer base. But the real question is, given all the changes to meta in recent months, do look-alikes still matter in 2026? The answer is yes, but a lot less than they used to. Look-alikes were a much bigger deal when meta needed more help figuring out who to target. But now, meta builds similar audience patterns in the background automatically. So, if you're feeding the platform good data through custom audiences, conversions, and engagement, it's already doing a lot of that work for you without you needing to go manually create a look-alike audience. That said, look-alikes can still help in certain cases, especially if you want to push meta harder towards a specific type of lead or customer, or you feel the algorithm is finding the wrong kind of prospect or what have you. So, they definitely still have use cases. They're just no longer the first lever most businesses should obsess over anymore. For most small business owners, custom audiences and clean campaign structure matter more. So, with that, I want to wrap up with why simpler account structures perform better for small businesses. This is the part most people need to hear. You probably do not need more campaigns. You probably probably do need a simpler account structure. Heavily segmented structures used to be more common. You'd run multiple campaigns at the same time and have a different ad set for each audience built off of detailed targeting options. But doing it that way now often just spreads your budget too thin. And when your budget gets split across too many audiences, meta gets less data in each pocket. That slows down meta's learning, and that slows optimization, making scaling harder. So, instead, you want to have one campaign, and within it one broader targeting ad set for prospecting, like we talked about, and one retargeting ad set using custom audiences, like we talked about. Setting your account up this way means less complexity, less maintenance, and faster learning from the budget you already have. So, just to recap everything quickly, use hard controls only for real limits. Use audience suggestions to guide meta. Build strong custom audiences from your actual business data. Use look-alikes when needed, but don't lean on them like it's 2020. And keep your structure simple enough that meta can actually learn. Because in 2026, the businesses getting results with meta ads are not usually the ones doing the most targeting. They're the ones giving meta the clearest signals. And if you do that well, you give your business a much better shot at generating more leads, more customers, and more predictable growth from the same ad spend.

Meta wants a clean, consistent experience from the ad to the landing page. Reason number three is restricted content categories. Certain industries trigger extra scrutiny. It's quite similar to how Google Ads auction works, where multiple advertisers bid for a user's attention, but only a few ads actually get displayed. From Facebook's perspective, what does it want? It wants advertisers to be able to run ads that reach the exact audience they're aiming for so that their businesses grow and their campaigns perform well. At the same time, Facebook also cares about user experience. It doesn't want users to be bombarded with irrelevant or excessive ads. Without this data, Facebook wouldn't know who's interested in travel, for example. So, when an advertiser wants to target travel enthusiasts, the ad wouldn't be delivered properly if the data wasn't accurate. That's why Facebook is constantly working on strengthening its database through acquisitions, developing new data sources, and refining accuracy. The goal is to make sure their targeting remains effective and trustworthy. This way advertisers make money, users get relevant ads and Facebook earns even more in the process. To maintain this well balanced system, Facebook relies heavily on collecting and refining data from a wide range of sources. So let's talk about the first major data source which is the advertisers themselves. Facebook gathers a lot of information through advertisers like what kind of targeting they're setting, what type of audience they're selecting, the landing pages they're using, and what kind of data their Facebook pixel is capturing. After all, the Facebook pixel is a part of Facebook's own ecosystem. All of this contributes to Facebook's overall data collection. But the most interesting data source for Facebook is actually you and me, the users. For example, if I update my profile and mention that I got married in 2024, Facebook immediately registers that activity. Now, if an advertiser comes in and wants to target people who've gotten married in the last 1 year, I'll be included in that audience. Similarly, when a user posts something like traveling to the USA as a status update and an advertiser wants to reach people currently traveling to the USA, Facebook connects the dots and includes that user in the ad targeting all because the user shared that information themselves. Each of these actions strengthens its data points and makes its ad targeting engine even more robust. So by now it's clear that data points play a huge role. Along with that, he also engages with digital marketing content, likes posts, comments on ads or joins groups related to digital marketing. Based on this behavior, Facebook concludes that this user is interested in both sports and digital marketing and that information is now stored in Facebook's database. Now, here's where the auction part begins. Facebook system is designed to track and store similar patterns for millions of users. So now that Facebook knows user X's interest, let's introduce a few advertisers. Say advertiser one sells wooden bats or sports accessories. Will all three ads be shown? Well, you might say yes, but here's the catch. Let's assume this user is on Facebook for just 1 minute. In that limited time, Facebook can't show every single ad. Now, you might say, okay, maybe the ad will be shown one after another, but even then there's competition. Why is one ad shown before the other? Why did advertiser one's ad appear first and not advertiser 3's? Even if we assume all three ads will eventually be shown since the user falls under all their target audiences, they won't all appear in the same spot at the same time. That's the key point. There's real competition happening between advertisers. Many advertisers are fighting for that same spot in the feed. So, what exactly is happening here? It's an auction. In this auction, the advertisers are the biders. They're bringing in their ads and placing bids to win visibility. And what's being sold? The ad space. The opportunity to show an ad in a user's feed. All three advertisers want that one spot, but only one will win. The winner's ad gets displayed first. If the user keeps scrolling, maybe the second and third ads will also appear, but nothing gets shown without going through an auction. Now, here's the interesting part. You might think advertiser once set a $10 budget. Advertiser two went with $20 and advertiser three with $30. So obviously the highest bidder will win. But that's not how it works. And this is where digital ad auctions become fascinating. It's not just about who bids the most. The auction system considers more than just money. If winning depended solely on who had the biggest budget, that wouldn't be fair. Social media platforms like Facebook can't allow the system to be entirely commercialized. It's designed to be balanced and fair for both advertisers and users. That's why Facebook's auction doesn't just reward the highest bidder. It evaluates several other factors to decide which ad will offer the best user experience, engagement potential, and relevance. So remember, in Facebook ads auction, the bid matters, but it's not everything. All right, there are a lot of things to consider here. For example, if advertiser 3 has money, they might want to show ads about shoes or even property to users who are just interested in sports. But that's against Facebook's policies. Why? Because every social media platform wants users to stay on the app longer, not leave. And that only happens when users have a good experience. If someone keeps seeing irrelevant ads all the time, they'll skip them or even stop using the app altogether. Unless the platform offers something really unique, people will eventually move away. So, Facebook designed its ad option system in such a way that money isn't the only deciding factor. It's made to keep things fair and ensure users don't get frustrated. So, now coming back to the main question, among the three advertisers, whose ad will actually be shown to the user? Well, technically any of the three ads can show up. It all depends on the auction. And now we know it's not just about who bids the most. In fact, Facebook uses a very solid formula called total value to decide the winner in this auction. This total value is made up of three parts. First is the bid, how much the advertiser is willing to pay. Second is the estimated action rate. This is where Facebook's algorithm predicts the probability of a user engaging with the ad. It calculates if I show this ad, is the user likely to click? And third is ad quality. Whether the ad follows Facebook's guidelines, whether the image is appropriate and how well that ad has performed in the past. If an ad keeps getting reported or gets low interaction, that's a red flag. So the ad that wins the auction based on this total value gets displayed. That's why even if you simply set your targeting on Facebook, the system behind it is extremely robust. This is how the entire ad auction ecosystem works between advertisers and users. - What Is Meta’s Opportunity Score? (And Why It Matters): Kind: captions Language: en Meta is always launching new features inside of Ads Manager. And to be honest, sometimes that roll out can be mysterious. But this time, I actually partnered with Meta to be able to interview one of their product marketing managers to ask all of our toughest questions about this new feature that is rolling out on Ads Manager right now called the opportunity score. Maybe a few of you already have early access. So, what is the opportunity score? Why does it matter? And is this just another way to get us to spend more money? I personally don't think so. I actually think that this feature is going to really affect how teams, particularly brands and agencies, work together. This update has really big implications for how we collaborate, how we optimize, and how we make decisions inside of Ads Manager. So, let's talk about what the opportunity score actually is. The opportunity score shows how optimized your ad account is to Meta's best practices from a score of 0 to 100. And now I'd like you to meet Sarah Reading, product marketing manager at Meta. So this is on a 0ero to 100 point scale and it represents how optimized campaigns, adsets, and ads are. Uh it can identify and rank proven recommendations that are personalized to a business's profile and their campaign characteristics. And a lot of these recommendations can be applied in just a few clicks. And these recommendations are proven, personalized, and even prioritized based on your business and campaign type. So first on proven, the tool only shows recommendations that have been proven to improve performance for similar advertisers. Also, the recommendations are personalized and the points are customized based on your business profile and campaign characteristics. And then lastly, on prioritized, uh the recommendations are ranked based on estimated value and impact. Over the years, we've heard that businesses really want more personalized guidance on how to get the most out of their campaigns really led us to um developing opportunity score. But here's what the opportunity score isn't. It's not a grade. It's not used in the auction. And it's candidly not something that you need to obsess over if you see your score dipping. I compare it to more like a credit score. So, let's talk about what type of recommendations you're going to see and what kind of things can actually affect your score. As of recording, there are over 60 possible recommendations currently inside of Ads Manager. And they fall into a number of categories: campaign type, audience, creative, data, and signals, budget and bidding, errors and warning, and the mysterious other. And here's where it gets really interesting, right? Some recommendations might actually carry more weight for your business as opposed to another depending on what industry you're in or even your account setup. And the number of points will be unique to each account. So it really will look at for you what could have potentially the most impact. So you may get a recommendation in an ad account that's worth 20 points because that is how much it could potentially lift your score versus I might see the same recommendation in my account but it's only worth 10 points because I'm doing different things in my account that mean that you have more opportunity than I do. One example is with creative fatigue. Meta might flag that your audience has seen the same ad a number of times and suggest that you swap it out. As a consumer, if you see the same ad 30 or 40 times, you probably are less likely to interact with it. Again, that doesn't mean that you're doing anything wrong with opportunity score. If you recommendation, if like your score drops and then you get a recommendation u that you're seeing creative fatigue and should potentially refresh your creative. And if that creative swap has high potential to boost your results, it might actually show up as more points for your particular account as opposed to someone else's. But what can advertisers expect? What is this going to look like? So, let's actually see what this looks like inside of one of my ad accounts. And for context, I actually work with this brand in a creative strategy capacity, not a media buying one. So, there were a few suggestions here where I was like, "Hey, actually, I'm going to sync with the media buyer on this." Like this one right here. you could get an 8% lower cost per result by optimizing your video format. And essentially what this says is, hey, you should be adding 9 by6 formats to your ads. And I was like, yeah, we should be doing that as a standard operating process. So, this was a really great conversation starter for me to connect with the creative team as well as the media buyer to make sure that we're actually doing that. And I know what you're dying to know. What is my opportunity score on this specific account, right? and it is actually a 64. I definitely would encourage businesses to not get discouraged by a score below 100 or even a score that is at 100 and then drops. It's really just a chance to try out more of our best practices because opportunity score does change in real time with the recommendations that it offers. As things in a campaign change like auction dynamics or creative fatigue, your score may drop, but that just means that we're showing you um another opportunity to potentially improve performance. So, if your score is in the 60s like mine or in the 70s or 80s or it even dips one day, it's not a sign to panic. It's actually just a sign to dig a little bit deeper and see what Meta has to say about keeping best practices inside of your ad account. So, here's my honest take. Whether you love it or hate it or you feel neutral about it, if you are working on a big team or you are agency side working directly with brands, you really need to understand how this new feature works. Okay, so the next point that's really important for new meta ad accounts is to differentiate with specificity. What do I mean by that? It's really difficult for you to compete with the bigger brands in your space. If your product or service to your prospects looks the same or very similar to theirs, because those people are just going to buy from your competitors that have the brand name. Now, there's obviously a number of different ways you can differentiate. You could differentiate by having the best price point and being less expensive than competitors. Often not ideal because that's going to eat into your margin and it's not great for the long-term viability of your business. One of the easiest ways to differentiate is with the specificity of your marketing message. You don't even necessarily need to have increased specificity in the product or service, but what you can do is instead of trying to create ads that speak to the whole market, break your market down into very specific avatars and then create ads to specifically speak to them. Because if you do that, people are much more likely to go, "Yes, that's me. I'll engage with this ad. I'll buy this product or service." So, let me give you an example, right? So, I've got an offer called the Ben Heath Inner Circle. And they can ask me questions and I can help them out and and give them specific advice. So, that's actually quite a broad offer, but we can break that market down, people who may be interested in that, into specific customer avatars and then create ads that really speak to those people. Same offer, different specific message. You could do the same thing with physical products. How might different people use your product? Break that down and then create ads for each specific use case. Which different types of people might be interested in your service? Is there residential versus commercial? Is there hobbyists at who are really keen versus complete beginners to the market? All sorts of different categorizations is going to very much depend on your business, but really do that and break that down and it's one of the easiest ways and most reliable ways to stand out from your competition. Because the big players are less likely to do that.
