Now, this next one is a big one. So many people are getting their conversion tracking wrong. It sounds obvious, but I'm actually talking about a specific case that I see fairly often. So, I'll give you some examples. So, typically the way it would work is someone clicks on your Meta ad, they come through to a landing page, that triggers the page view event, then they go ahead and purchase and they would get the purchase conversion event that would usually load up on an order confirmation page, or they inquire, they become a lead, they go through to a a thank you page after they submitted their information and the lead conversion event would be triggered on that thank you page. But sometimes, advertisers or people setting this up will get this wrong and the purchase or the lead conversion event will be triggered on the landing [clears throat] page load up. So, basically, anyone that clicks off an ad from Facebook or Instagram comes through to a landing page, whether they go on to convert or not, a purchase or a lead is registered. That is a bit of a disaster because that tells Meta that everyone that clicks through becomes a purchase or a lead and Meta therefore goes, "Great, we just need to send this page as much traffic as possible." Meta won't be able to learn and work out who does convert and who doesn't. Who should we put ads in front of, what time of day, what placement options, all the various things that Meta's going to do to optimize your campaigns if you have conversion tracking set up properly. Instead, they're just going to send as many people as possible. That'll include spam accounts, that'll include bots, not stuff that you want at all. You're actually much better off with a Meta pixel that doesn't know how to get you results than one that thinks it is getting you results when it actually isn't. It's more of a common issue than you think. The easiest way to check it is to use this free Chrome extension, which is the Meta Pixel Helper. When you use that, you can go into the various pages on your website, and you can see which conversion events, which events are being triggered on page load, and just absolutely double-check that on the landing page that you're sending traffic through to, that nothing else is being registered as an event other than just simple page view. So, your conversion rates from people that find out about your product or service, and then click through, are going to be higher because they've been recommended by someone that people within your target audience know, like, and trust. A skill that a lot of advertisers, even more established businesses that are just starting running Meta ads with a new ad account, don't often have. Okay, my next point is to optimize for what you really want. And this point is especially important for new Meta ad accounts because there is so much bad advice out there about this. You'll probably have heard this already, that with a new Meta ad account, you can't optimize for purchases, you can't optimize for leads, you can't use the sales or leads campaign objective, you have to go with traffic, send people through to your website and warm up the pixel, or you have to go with engagement and warm up the pixel. All absolute nonsense. It is true that new Meta ad accounts are not as good at generating sales and leads, the thing that you most likely want, as more experienced ad accounts, because Meta doesn't have as much conversion data that they can use to make all sorts of decisions and optimizations around who to target within your target audience, what time of day to put ads in front of them, is it better to put an ad on Instagram stories versus Facebook Reels. Much better off to run a conversion-based campaign, so like leads or sales, and get the results that you want and actually have the system learn. The optimization system within Meta is very literal. If you say you want link clicks, Meta will get you link clicks. And Meta won't think it's not doing what you want if those link clicks come from fake accounts, from bots. Meta's like, "Hey, these are link clicks. You wanted link clicks, right? We've got them for you." So, you have to be smarter as an advertiser and just not listen to that terrible advice that's been out there and just won't go away, and actually think, "Okay, this is what I want. This is what I'm going to optimize for." The vast, vast majority of advertisers, especially if you're sending people through to your website, should be going with the leads or sales campaign objectives. And then, as your performance goal, because you can mess up a good campaign objective selection by getting the performance goal wrong, the performance goal, make sure you're optimizing for the conversion event that you really want, usually leads or purchases. Here's another new piece of advice for new Meta ad accounts that very few advertisers actually follow. And that's to start with just one campaign and one ad set. You could have multiple ads within that ad set, but that is what I want you to start with for your campaign structure. Let's keep things really simple. As the platform has evolved and matured and become more sophisticated, we're moving more and more towards that setup even with more experienced ad accounts, but especially at the beginning I think it's really key. Firstly, new Meta ad accounts when you're getting started typically don't operate with large budgets. That means you're not going to be generating tons of conversions most likely. If you separate that budget across lots of different campaigns, really hard for Meta to learn, for you to get out of the learning phase, really hard for you as an advertiser to draw conclusions. If everything's going through the one option, then you can learn a lot faster and Meta can also learn a lot faster. I also think it really helps with focus. So, in that one campaign, what I would normally recommend, like just get started offering the one thing, the one product or product range, and the one service, your best seller or your most profitable offering. Keep things nice and simple early on. And along those same lines, it's really important that you respect the learning phase. So, the learning phase usually lasts 24 to 48 hours, but can vary significantly. And it occurs after you launch a new ad set or make significant adjustments to an ad set. And during that time period, Meta is going into hyper learning mode, testing who within your target audience is likely to deliver the best results, placement options, all those various things, right? So, results are very volatile. Meta continues to learn after the learning phase, by the way, but they're doing the more foundational heavy testing right at the beginning. So, results are likely to go up and down. What a lot of advertisers with new ad accounts cannot do is just leave their ad campaigns alone and give them a bit of time. So, it's really important that you give yourself I would try and come up with a fixed optimization schedule of like I'm going to launch stuff, I'm going to give it 5 days, I'm going to give it 7 days, I'm going to give it 10 days, then I'm going to assess, then I'm going to make a suite of changes, whether that's introducing new ads or tweaking other things in one go. I'll go back into the learning phase, I'll then give it more time, and then I'll assess the results. The length of time does vary because of your conversion volume. If you're generating just a few conversions a week, it might take months for you to know which ad is performing best and the correct adjustments to make. If, however, you're generating 200 purchases a day, well, you might be able to make decisions that same day. So, it very much depends on conversion volume. Try and establish a time frame and don't constantly tinker, otherwise you're constantly in the learning phase and you will hurt your results. So, my next point is very much overlooked by a lot of advertisers, not really understood. And that's it's really important to understand what your results mean. So, you've got your new Meta ad account, you've got your campaign, hopefully just the one, up and running, and you start generating results. And then what advertisers will then do based on the results is they will just kind of guess at what they should do next. You want to be much more intentional than that. So, I'm going to run you through a quick framework after some of the most common scenarios and what action you should take based on what you see. So, if you're not generating any conversions at all, it's most likely an offer problem. People aren't interested enough in what you have to offer at the price point in order to go ahead and purchase or become a lead. That's typical. A lot of advertisers in that scenario think it's a targeting problem. That's unlikely nowadays. Meta's so good at working out who to put your ads in front of. It's most likely an offer problem. You need to differentiate from your competition. How can you speed up the deliverable? How can you reduce the risk for your prospects? Guarantees, things like that. Is there anything you can do on price? How can you deliver more value? How can you make that clearer? An offer problem. My recommendation for how to improve offers is Alex Hormozi's 100 million dollar offers book, which is absolutely fantastic. Scenario number two. Let's say you're generating some conversions, not very many, less than you would like, but you've got a low hook rate and you've got a low click-through rate. So, you've got some conversions, like people are interested in your offer, but your ads aren't doing a good job enough of grabbing their attention in the first place, holding it, and then convincing them to take action. Ad creative problem in that scenario. Okay, next scenario. We just need to adjust the stuff that happens either with pricing or post that initial purchase. But yeah, model problem in that scenario. Next scenario, the one we were on, lots of conversions and profitable, in which case, scale. Again, not all advertisers will do this. I've had lots of conversations with people being like, "The results look good. Should I spend more?" And I'm like, "Yes, absolutely." Of course we can improve things as you go along, but if it's working well and you have capacity to deliver on that product or deliver on that service, then go ahead and and scale. One thing that's really important, and this happens a lot to advertisers with new ad accounts that are new to the platform, is that unless they're in that last scenario, where things are great, lots of conversions and profitable, they run their ads and they don't get conversions and they get some conversions but not enough and they just assume Meta ads don't work. In this video, I'm going to show you exactly how to do it. So, the feature that allows you to do what I just described is called value rules. And with value rules, you're able to optimize for different demographics within your target audience. So, instead of just saying to Meta, "Go and get me as many conversions as possible, as many leads or sales as possible, or even get me as much value as possible optimizing for the highest return on ad spend." Instead, you can say to Meta, "I would rather you place a priority on these people, this group, this demographic within my overall target audience because I know they are more valuable to my business." How does Gem and and Lattice, which are two other products or or whatever we call them, services, products that that Meta has developed over really 2025 and into 26, how are they playing in this Andromeda era? >> That's a great question. So, uh Lattice is effectively our model consolidation technology. Previously, you would have a variation of a machine learning model that was extremely specific. This model is predicting clicks on Facebook. This model is predicting clicks on Instagram. This model is predicting conversions on Facebook. This model is predicting conversions on Instagram. It turns out that if you take all of those machine learning models and you collapse them into one model all of the training data that used to go into those four models into the one model, the performance of the one model is better than the performance of each of the individual models because that larger model is able to learn from more data, more of your interests, more of your interactions with businesses, products, services, et cetera. And that just improves the performance for the advertiser as well as the experience for the person who is seeing an ad. You're going to see a more relevant ad because we consolidated these models and the training data used on these models to drive better outcomes for advertisers. So, that's Lattice. Gem is a quite a bit further expression of that where we're creating this large generative recommendation model that is really our foundation model that all of our other models kind of learn from in that kind of second stage ranking environment. And then we try to take that super large foundation model, the generative ads recommendation model, and we build a LLM scale and complexity inference model out of that foundation model and that's what we started calling the adaptive ranking model where some number of people interact with brands and content a lot, have a lot of interest, they interact a lot on the platform, they purchase more than other folks, they have longer sequences of interactions, we can learn more from about them and make better recommendations for them from their more more interactions on the platform. Other folks, they don't interact as much as brands, they don't purchase as much. They have shorter interaction sequences, interaction histories. So we don't need to use as much compute in recommending an ad for them because there's just not as much input data. And so adaptive ranking model allows us to dramatically scale up on the kind of power user segment and produce substantial improvements in performance for those users who are engaging a lot with brands and content etc. >> Woah, okay. Super good explanation. I've not heard that explained. And it's really important one that goes to how the auction actually works. So, because we are running a very large number of auctions in real time every second, and you have a very large number of bidders who are bidding every second, the more advertisers on the platform, and the more frequently they change their bid prices or bid volume, the more changes that everyone sees in the auction dynamics that play out in the marketplace. It's like the stock market. There are a bunch of different tickers, a bunch of people, retail investors, institutional investors, they're bidding up the prices of the stocks or down the prices of the stocks in real time as they think those stocks are more or less valuable to those investors. The same effect is playing out in the advertising auction. There are a bunch of people, and maybe some information comes in that this customer is much more likely to be interested in the product or service you're offering, maybe you increase your bid because they would purchase from you if they saw your ad. Or maybe some new information comes in, and they just bought a refrigerator, they're unlikely to buy a new refrigerator soon, and that customer is no longer as valuable to you, and you change your bid. So, as better information propagates throughout the ecosystem, more advertisers are changing their bids more frequently based on what they learn about customers and their desires. So, those bid changes at volume create the volatility that people experience on their campaigns cuz it's a dynamic marketplace that is optimized for delivering the best price for each advertiser. And so, as you change your bid, different people and conversions will appear to you, not just because of the changes that you've made, but because of the changes that everybody else is making in response. Uh so, volatility and uh associated price optimization are the opposite end of the spectrum of low volatility and higher prices. So, you kind of have to decide which matters more to you, lowest prices or um low volatility, cuz they're on opposite ends of the spectrum. >> Uh which is like essentially the more you're spending and the more conversions you're driving, the less overall volatility you will have because in a very simple term, the algorithm will be more confident in the decisions that it's putting in front of you. And then one or two or 100 people or 1,000 people that may have been considered to purchase that drop out of your funnel because there's a signal like they bought that refrigerator, that's not impacting your total funnel. When you're spending less or when your average order values or cost per acquisitions are significantly higher, you then therefore have to just accept that you have to deal with volatility because the algorithm isn't just going to endlessly keep spending on people who are no longer actually a part of your your true purchase funnel anymore. >> Yep. It's the difference between a highly profitable campaign and a loss-making one. And the vast majority of advertisers don't know how that auction system works. In this video, I'm going to show you a super simple way to understand how the auction in MetaCache really works so you can get the most out of it and maximize the profitability of your campaigns. In the auction, everyone competes, all advertisers who share the same target audience. So, when Jose in this case suddenly has an ad space within the inventory, it doesn't matter if it's in stories, in the feed, in the explore section, wherever, you know we have many placements within meta, this real-time auction takes place which ranks the advertisers. Who wins this auction? Well, in a traditional auction , whoever pushes the hardest wins, right? The first factor in winning this auction is indeed the bid. Meta prioritizes user experience and the advertiser's experience, which obviously also has to be good, because if the advertiser doesn't have a good experience, they will withdraw their investment from Meta and take it to another platform. There are two more important factors within the auction. First, the percentage of estimated shares. This is basically the probability that when an advertisement is shown to a person, in this case Jose, Jose will produce the result that the advertiser expects. In other words, if I'm using a Lits acquisition campaign, what Meta will do is prioritize my ad, showing my ad to the people who, with all the information and all the signals that Meta has, are most likely to take that conversion action. In this case, please leave me your details. If I 'm optimizing a campaign for sales and I'm optimizing for the purchase event, Meta will try to prioritize, showing my ad to the people who are most likely to perform that conversion action, which in this case is the purchase. And we compete competitively with all inventory, across all target inventory, and with all advertisers vying for that inventory. This is one of the most important parts of this auction system, but then we have a very important factor which is the quality of the listing. The quality of the ad, after all , I always say that the ad is like the main point of contact between all the work we do as online advertising professionals and the user. What's going on ? There is one metric that is usually quite affected, which is the CPM, meaning our cost per 1000 impressions increases, so with the same budget we reach fewer people. Ad quality is basically the engagement that the person has with our ad, what the post-click experience is, that is, when the user clicks on an ad, does he/she convert, does not convert, spends time on the website, does not spend time on the website. In other words, it's the entire experience the user has when they come into contact with the ad. You have to keep in mind that these three factors are very, very important. The bid, the estimated percentage of shares, and the quality of the ad. But it's important to keep in mind that the estimated share percentage and ad quality take precedence over the bid. In fact, this metric, the combination of these two metrics, is called relevance score, which is basic, as we said at the beginning, that our ads have to be relevant to the audience, because if they are not relevant to the audience, the user will leave. So, what measures relevance? First, that the percentage of estimated shares is high. And secondly, that the quality of the advertisement is high, that it is good. And this, as I mentioned, takes priority over the bid. And that's how the metach auction system works. Here we're going to look at it in a different way. On the far left, what do we see? The total value. This is the total value we will be bidding on in the auction. The higher the total value in that auction, which takes place in real time and in milliseconds, the more priority we will have , the more preference we will have over other advertisers. So, how is that total value calculated? So we have the bid and the bid is multiplied by the sum of the estimated percentage of shares and the user's value. It performs an internal meta-calculation and provides that total value. If we have a higher total value, we will win more auctions, which will make us much more competitive and, most importantly, much more profitable. And that's it .
